Illustration
8.11
(a) is a depiction of
S
.
Illustration
8.11
(b) depicts the gradual exchange process that we developed earlier in each window. For each window, we explored earlier that
S^{\prime}
is a direct sum (in a rotated basis) of weighted shift operators whose orbits are broken in the window. Because we do this in each window, we can piece together these orbits.
Illustrations
8.12
(a) and
8.12
(b) illustrate these orbits. We then use these orbits to construct projections
E_{j}
so that
A^{\prime}
has spectral projections
E_{j}
. Because each orbit belongs to at most two consecutive windows,
A^{\prime}
will be approximately equal to
A
if the window length is small.

For each orbit, we construct a nearby normal using Theorem
7.3.1
. Then putting these normals together gives
S^{\prime\prime}
.

We repeat the notation from the previous lemma in the statement of the next lemma. This result completes the construction of nearby commuting matrices using the gradual exchange process. The use of projections to construct nearby commuting matrices is motivated by the constructions in
[
61
,
33
]
.

Lemma 8.3.4
.

Let
A_{r}=\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_{i}),S_{r}=\operatorname{ws}(c_{i}^{r})
with respect to some orthonormal basis of
M_{n_{r}}(\mathbb{C})
for
r=1,\dots,m
and
i=\underline{i}_{r},\dots,\overline{i}_{r}
, where
[\underline{i}_{r},\overline{i}_{r}]\subset[\underline{i}_{r+1},\overline{i}_{r+1}]
.
Suppose that the
\alpha_{i}
are real and strictly increasing. Define
A=\bigoplus_{r}A_{r},S=\bigoplus_{r}S_{r}
. Let
\mathscr{R}_{I}=\{r:\sigma(A)\cap I\subset\sigma(A_{r})\}
. Consequently,
\mathscr{R}_{I}
is empty or equal to
r_{0},r_{0}+1,\dots,m
for some
r_{0}=r_{0}(I)\geq 1
which may depend on
I
.

Let
a_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
,
a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{n_{0}}
,
I_{k}=[a_{k},a_{k+1})
for
k+1<n_{0}
and
I_{n_{0}-1}=[a_{n_{0}-1},a_{n_{0}}]
, satisfying
\sigma(A)\subset\bigcup_{k}I_{k}
.
Let
m_{k}=m+1-r_{0}(I_{k})\leq m
and let
N_{I_{k}}=N_{k}
be natural numbers such that

\displaystyle\#\sigma(A)\cap I_{k}\geq\max\left(3,(2m_{k}-3)(N_{k}+1)+4\right).

(8.8)

Let

\displaystyle G_{I}

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}r<m\\
r\in\mathscr{R}_{I}\end{subarray}}\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}\left(||c_{i}^{r+1}|-|c_{i}^{r}||+\frac{\pi}{2N_{I}}\max(|c_{i}^{r}|,|c_{i}^{r+1}|)\right)

(8.9)

\displaystyle D_{I}

\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}|c_{i}^{r_{0}}|

(8.10)

\displaystyle T_{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{I}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}r<m\\
r\in\mathscr{R}_{I}\end{subarray}}\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}||c_{i}^{r+1}|^{2}-|c_{i}^{r}|^{2}|.

(8.11)

Then there is a self-adjoint matrix
A^{\prime}
commuting with a matrix
S^{\prime}
that is a direct sum of weighted shift matrices in an eigenbasis of
A^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime}-A\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k}\operatorname{diam}I_{k},

(8.12)

\displaystyle\|S^{\prime}-S\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k}\max(G_{I_{k}},D_{I_{k}}),\noindent

(8.13)

\displaystyle\|\,[S^{\prime\ast},S^{\prime}]\,\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k}\max\left(\|[S^{\ast},S]\|+T_{I_{k}},\,D_{I_{k}}^{2}\right).

(8.14)

Moreover, there is a normal
S^{\prime\prime}
that is a direct sum of weighted shift matrices in an eigenbasis of
A^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\|S^{\prime\prime}-S^{\prime}\|\leq C_{\alpha}\|S\|^{1-2\alpha}\|\,[S^{\prime\ast},S^{\prime}]\,\|^{\alpha}

(8.15)

where
\alpha,C_{\alpha}>0
are constants such that a nearby normal matrix can be obtained by Theorem
7.3.1
.

If the
c_{i}^{r}
are real then using
\alpha=1/3,C_{1/3}=5.3308
allows
S^{\prime\prime}
to be real.
Moreover, there is a real change of basis that makes
S^{\prime\prime}
(and also
S^{\prime}
) a direct sum of weighted shift matrices with real weights.

Remark 8.3.5
.

If we estimate

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{I}

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}r<m\\
r\in\mathscr{R}_{I}\end{subarray}}\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}||c_{i}^{r+1}|-|c_{i}^{r}||,

\displaystyle R_{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2N}\max_{r\in\mathscr{R}_{I}}\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}|c_{i}^{r}|

separately then we obtain the bounds for
G_{I}
:
\max(\varepsilon_{I},R_{I})\leq G_{I}\leq\varepsilon_{I}+R_{I}
.

Proof.

Construction of and estimates for
A^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}
: Let
a^{\sigma}_{k}=\min\sigma(A)\cap[a_{k},a_{k+1})
and
b^{\sigma}_{k}=\max\sigma(A)\cap[a_{k},a_{k+1})
.
Let
F_{k}
be the projection gotten by applying the construction in Lemma
8.3.1
for
[a^{\sigma}_{k},b^{\sigma}_{k}]
, let
S_{k}^{\prime}
be the constructed perturbation of
S
, and
F_{k}^{c}=E_{[a^{\sigma}_{k},b^{\sigma}_{k}]}(A)-F_{k}
.
Note that
E_{\{b^{\sigma}_{k}\}}(A)\leq F_{k}^{c}\leq E_{(a^{\sigma}_{k},b^{\sigma}_{k}]}(A)
.
Define

S^{\prime}=S+\sum_{k}(S^{\prime}_{k}-S).

The definition that we give here for
S^{\prime}
is the same as applying all these perturbations from the previous lemma in each window separately. Because the perturbations
S^{\prime}_{k}-S
are supported on and have range in the orthogonal subspaces
R(E_{I_{k}}(A))
, we obtain the desired estimate for
\|S^{\prime}-S\|
.

Consider the orthogonal projections
E_{k}
defined to be the

F_{1},F_{1}^{c}+F_{2},\dots,F_{k}^{c}+F_{k+1},\dots,F_{n_{0}-1}^{c}+F_{n_{0}},F_{n_{0}}^{c}.

Because the
F_{k}
are invariant under
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}
maps
R(E_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}(A))
into
R(E_{[a_{k},a^{\sigma}_{k+1}]}(A))
,
we see that
S^{\prime}
maps
R(F_{k}^{c})
into
R(F_{k}^{c})+R(F_{k+1})
.
Hence, the projections
E_{k}
commute with
S^{\prime}
.
Note that
E_{k}\leq E_{[a_{k-1},a_{k+1}]}(A)
if
a_{0}
is defined to be
a_{1}
and
a_{n_{0}+1}
is defined to be
a_{n_{0}}
. So, letting
A^{\prime}=\sum_{k}a_{k}E_{k}
, we see that
[S^{\prime},A^{\prime}]=0
and
\|A^{\prime}-A\|\leq\max_{k}(a_{k+1}-a_{k})
.

Construction of and estimates for
S^{\prime\prime}
: We now take advantage of the structure of
S^{\prime}
through the operators
S^{\prime}_{k}
, which were called
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{M}}
in the proof of Lemma
8.3.1
. Please recall the construction of what was called
S^{\prime}
in Lemma
8.2.2
, in particular the statement about the support and range of
S^{\prime}-S
illustrated in Equations (
8.5
) and (
8.6
). These contribute to the construction of each
S^{\prime}_{k}
.

We know that
S^{\prime}
is a direct sum of weighted shift operators. Because the construction of
S^{\prime}_{k}
in each window did not change the weights of the weighted shifts on the boundaries, we see that the differences of the squares of the
S^{\prime}
weights between windows are the same as those of
S
between windows. Within windows, the differences of squares of
S^{\prime}
weights are bounded by the estimates for the self-commutator of the
S_{k}^{\prime}
in Lemma
8.3.1
. So, the desired estimate for the self-commutator of
S^{\prime}
holds.

Because
S^{\prime}
commutes with
A^{\prime}
, we can view the orbits of
S^{\prime}
as lying within the eigenspaces of
A^{\prime}
. We then apply Theorem
7.3.1
to each such weighted shift orbit to obtain
S^{\prime\prime}
. If the
c_{i}^{r}
are real then the additional structure follows from that of Lemma
8.3.1
.

∎

Remark 8.3.6
.

We now discuss the utility of the estimates gotten in this construction.

We first discuss the term
D_{I}
.
Under some mild conditions, we need the singular values
\min_{r}\min_{\alpha_{i}\in I}|c_{i}^{r}|
to be small in order for there to exist structured nearby commuting matrices by a generalization of Voiculescu’s argument in
[
122
]
. This suggests that the estimate of
D_{I}=\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}|c_{i}^{r_{0}}|
might be small for situations where we want to construct nearby commuting matrices.

The construction in Lemma
8.2.2
strictly speaking does not make use of the fact that all
|c_{i}^{r_{0}}|
are small for
\alpha_{i}\in I
since only
m
weights are set equal to zero in the construction of the invariant subspace. A different choice of which weights to set equal to zero based on the particular problem at hand might be able to improve this estimate when the values of
|c_{i}^{r}|
vary rapidly in
i
. However, if each
S_{r}
is almost normal then we expect such variation to be controlled by the self-commutator of
S
.

We now discuss the term
G_{I}
. This term is a consequence of the application of the gradual exchange lemma to consecutive weighted shift operators
S_{t},S_{t-1}
.
Based on the details of this construction, the term
G_{I}
can be changed by reordering the weighted shift operators
S_{r_{1}}
,
S_{r_{2}}
in the direct sum given that
A_{r_{1}}=A_{r_{2}}
. In our application to Ogata’s theorem in the next chapter, the weights
c_{i}^{r}
will be increasing in
r
so the natural ordering based on the spin of the representations is optimal.

The only contribution to
G_{I}
that depends explicitly on
A
is the appearance of the
N_{I}
in the term corresponding to
R_{I}
. In applications, we will choose the points
a_{i}
first so that then
N_{I}
is chosen to be as large as possible.
There is a trade-off between how small the spacing of the
a_{i}
can be and how large
N_{I}
can be. The spacing of the
a_{i}
may directly affect all the terms
\varepsilon_{I},R_{I},G_{I},D_{I}
while the size of
N_{I}
only directly affects
R_{I}
.

Because we assume that
[A,S]
is small, we know that

|\alpha_{i+1}-\alpha_{i}||c_{i}^{r}|\leq\|[A,S]\|

is small. Assuming that the norm of
S
on
E_{I}(A)
is of order
1
, we know that
\max_{\alpha_{i}\in I}|c_{i}^{r}|
is bounded and so
|\alpha_{i+1}-\alpha_{i}|
is at most a constant multiple of
\|[A,S]\|
.
So, we choose the
a_{i}
so that
\operatorname{diam}I_{k}
is much larger than the spacing of the eigenvalues of
A
and hence
N_{I}
is large. Exactly how large
N_{I}
will be will depend on the situation, but we will want balance the size of the various components of the estimate to obtain the optimal result.

We now discuss the term
T_{I}
. The norm of the self-commutator of
S
,
\|[S^{\ast},S]\|
, reflects the sizes of the differences of the squares of the absolute values of the weights of
S
along individual orbits. When applying the gradual exchange lemma, we then need to take into account that the weights of
S_{t},S_{t-1}
are blended together. The term
T_{I}
reflects the size of the differences of the squares of the absolute values of the weights of
S
between the consecutive orbits of
S_{t},S_{t-1}
, reduced by the factor
N_{I}^{-1}
due to how many vectors we have to smooth out the weights over.
So, we expect that if the weights of the weights shifts
S_{r}
do not vary much in
r
then
T_{I}
should not be too large.

Remark 8.3.7
.

As discussed previously, given any collection of
A_{r},S_{r}
, we can refine the direct sum over all
r
by partitioning the set of possible values of
r
then apply this lemma to each partition of direct summands separately.

An example of why one might want to do this is that it is easily possible that
m
is comparable to (or even larger than)
\#\sigma(A)
. In this case,
N_{I}
cannot be large so the estimate of
R_{I}
is not small.
Conversely, making the refinements too sparse conversely may increase the size of
\varepsilon_{I}
and
T_{I}
.

For instance, take any non-trivial example of
A,S
and repeatedly form direct sums with themselves. Having repeated summands only makes the estimate for
\|S^{\prime}-S\|
worse. This is because none of the estimates from the lemma change if the repeated summands are listed together in the lemma except that
N_{I}
necessarily must decrease due to the increase of
m
.

This sort of difficulty is relevant for our application to Ogata’s theorem.
In fact, it is on its face impossible to use this result without refinement for Ogata’s theorem as in the next chapter due to the
N
-fold tensor product of
S^{1/2}
being decomposed into many more than
N
subrepresentations.
Our approach in the next chapter will be to refine the direct sum to then apply this lemma.
We also obtain optimal results using the only freedom we have in this construction: the partition chosen and the windows
I_{k}
.

Chapter 9
Main Theorem

We assume that
\lambda_{1}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{m}
.
In Lemma
9.1.1
we will obtain nearby commuting self-adjoint matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
for
A_{i}=\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\lambda_{m}}(\sigma_{i})
.

Let
A_{r}=\operatorname{diag}(i/N)
for
-\lambda_{r}\leq i\leq\lambda_{r}
and
S_{r}=\operatorname{ws}(d_{\lambda_{r},i}/N)
for
-\lambda_{r}\leq i<\lambda_{r}
. Then for
A=\bigoplus_{r}A_{r}
and
S=\bigoplus_{r}S_{r}
, we have that
A_{1}=\operatorname{Re}(S)
,
A_{2}=\operatorname{Im}(S)
, and
A_{3}=A
. The proof of Lemma
9.1.1
relies upon using the estimates in Lemma
5.1.1
for the construction from Lemma
8.3.4
. We later optimize the result by choosing the lengths of the intervals
I_{j}
optimally.

Dividing by
N
here is referred to “normalizing” these operators.
For the moment we will focus only on the unnormalized weights
d_{\lambda_{r},i}
and unnormalized spectrum.
We assume that both
\lambda_{1}
and the maximum gap between the
\lambda_{r}
are not too small but also not too large.
See Illustration
9.1
.

Illustration 9.1:
Illustration of the weights
d_{\lambda_{r},i}
for
\lambda_{r}=25,30,35,\dots,100
.

For this discussion, and hence the proof of Ogata’s theorem, the estimates obtained in Lemma
5.1.1
for
d_{\lambda,i}
are central to the calculation of the estimates for the nearby commuting matrices and influence the use of words such as “small” and “large”.

When calculating the estimate for
D_{I}
,

Illustration 9.2:
Illustration of Illustration
9.1
focused on a small unnormalized interval
I=[-12,12]
near
0
.

one is concerned with the largest value of the weight of the representation
S^{\lambda_{r_{0}}}(\sigma_{+})
in the interval
I
, where
r=r_{0}
is the smallest index so that the spectrum of
S^{\lambda_{r}}(\sigma_{3})
spans the interval
I
.
See Illustration
9.2
for an interval near
0
. In this example,
r_{0}=1
and
D_{I}
corresponds to the largest (unnormalized) weight of
S^{\lambda_{1}}
, which is about
25
.

In the proof of our extension of Ogata’s theorem later in this paper, representations
S^{\lambda}
with small values of
\lambda
need to be dealt with separately due to the distribution of the multiplicities of the irreducible subrepresentations of the tensor representation. The reason that
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}
cannot be made very small and hence reduce the size of the
\varepsilon_{I}
contribution to
G_{I}
is also that it requires
m
to be very large.

As another example, consider the interval illustrated in Illustration
9.3
that is not near
0
or the boundary of the spectrum of
S^{\lambda_{m}}
.

Illustration 9.3:
Illustration of Illustration
9.1
focused on a small unnormalized interval
I=[-82,-60]
away from
0
.

In this case,
S^{\lambda_{r_{0}}}
corresponds to the arc passing the vertical axis a little more than
60
. For each
r<r_{0}
, the spectrum of
S^{\lambda_{r}}(\sigma_{3})
does not span the interval and for each
r\geq r_{0}
the spectrum does span the interval.

Because the gradual exchange process will be applied for all
r\geq r_{0}
, the estimate for
D_{I}
will involve the largest weight of
S^{\lambda_{0}}(\sigma_{+})
, which is slightly larger than
60
.
For an interval in this position, it is important that the length of the interval not be too large since although the smallest weight of
S^{\lambda_{r_{0}}}(\sigma_{+})
may be small, its largest weight may be large based on the growth of the weights within an orbit.
The length of the interval and the spacing of the
\lambda_{r}
give an inequality of the form
|\lambda_{r_{0}}-|i||\leq M
so that
D_{I}
is controlled by Lemma
5.1.1
(ii)
.

In this illustration, the smallest weight of
S^{\lambda_{r_{0}}}(\sigma_{+})
is about 25 and if the interval were extended to the right, the largest weight of
S^{\lambda_{r_{0}}}(\sigma_{+})
would grow. If the interval were only extended to the left, then at some point
r_{0}
would necessarily increase by multiples of
5
which then increases the largest weight of
S^{\lambda_{r_{0}}}(\sigma_{+})
to about
70
and so on.
So, we see that the length of the interval
I
cannot be too large. Alternatively, the length of
I
cannot be too small since then the spectrum of the
S^{\lambda_{r}}(\sigma_{3})
in that interval will be small. So, the
R_{I}
contribution to
G_{I}
will be large through
N_{I}
being small. These estimates get larger the farther this interval is from
0
.

9.1
Technical Lemmas

We now proceed to constructing nearby commuting matrices with various parameters in the estimates.

Lemma 9.1.1
.

Let
S=\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\lambda_{m}}
where
S^{\lambda}
is the irreducible
(2\lambda+1)
-dimensional spin representation of
su(2)
with

0\leq\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}\leq L,\lambda_{m}=\Lambda
and the
2\lambda_{r}
are all even or all odd.
Let
l,\Delta>0
with
4\leq N\Delta\leq 2\Lambda
.

Then there are commuting self-adjoint matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\|A_{1}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{1})\|,\|A_{2}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\max(G,D)+C_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\Lambda+1/2}{N}\right)^{1-2\alpha}\max(T^{\alpha},D^{2\alpha}),

\displaystyle\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\Delta},

(9.1)

where

\displaystyle c_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Lambda}{N\lfloor 2\Lambda/N\Delta\rfloor}\leq\frac{2\Lambda}{2\Lambda\Delta^{-1}-N}

(9.2)

\displaystyle N_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\lfloor\frac{N\Delta-5}{2m-3}\right\rfloor-1\geq\frac{N\Delta-5}{2(m-1)-1}-2

(9.3)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\left(2+\frac{2L}{N_{0}}\right)\frac{\Lambda}{N^{2}}

(9.4)

\displaystyle G=\frac{1}{N}\max\left(\sqrt{\Lambda}\frac{2L}{\sqrt{l}}+\frac{\pi}{2N_{0}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left(\Lambda+1/2\right),\sqrt{2\Lambda L}+\frac{\pi}{2N_{0}}\sqrt{2\Lambda(l+1)}\right)

(9.5)

\displaystyle D=\max\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\Lambda}{N}\left(\frac{L+1}{N}+c_{\Delta}\right)},\frac{\lambda_{1}+1/2}{N},\frac{c_{\Delta}}{2}+\frac{L+1/2}{N}\right),

(9.6)

\alpha\in(0,1/2],C_{\alpha}>0
are constants as in Theorem
7.3.1
, and
A_{3}^{\prime}
is real. Consequently, when using
\alpha=1/3,C_{1/3}=5.3308
, we have that
A_{1}^{\prime},iA_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}
are real.

Proof.

We wish to apply Lemma
8.3.4
with

A_{r}=\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{r}}(\sigma_{3})=\operatorname{diag}\left(-\frac{\lambda_{r}}{N},\frac{-\lambda_{r}+1}{N},\dots,\frac{\lambda_{r}}{N}\right)

S_{r}=\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{r}}(\sigma_{+})=\operatorname{ws}\left(\frac{d_{\lambda_{r},-\lambda_{r}}}{N},\frac{d_{\lambda_{r},-\lambda_{r}+1}}{N},\dots,\frac{d_{\lambda_{r},\lambda_{r}-1}}{N}\right)

so that

A_{r}=\operatorname{diag}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right),\;i=-\lambda_{r},-\lambda_{r}+1,\dots,\lambda_{r}

S_{r}=\operatorname{ws}\left(\frac{d_{\lambda_{r},i}}{N}\right),\;i=-\lambda_{r},-\lambda_{r}+1,\dots,\lambda_{r}-1.

Set
A=\bigoplus_{r}A_{r}
and
S=\bigoplus_{r}S_{r}
and
\alpha_{i}=i/N
,
c_{i}^{r}=d_{\lambda_{r},i}/N\geq 0
in accordance with the assumptions of Lemma
8.3.4
.
So, the estimates of
c_{i}^{r}
and
c_{i}^{r+1}-c_{i}^{r}
needed to apply Lemma
8.3.4
will be obtained from the inequalities for
d_{\lambda_{r},i}
and
d_{\lambda_{r+1},i}-d_{\lambda_{r},i}
in Lemma
5.1.1
.
We will then obtain nearby commuting
A^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}
is Hermitian and
S^{\prime\prime}
is normal. We then set
A_{1}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Re}(S^{\prime\prime}),A_{2}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Im}(S^{\prime\prime}),
and
A_{3}^{\prime}=A^{\prime}
.

We choose an increasing sequence of real numbers
a_{i}
to satisfy the conditions of Lemma
8.3.4
with
a_{1}=-\Lambda/N
and
a_{n_{0}}=\Lambda/N
satisfying

\displaystyle a_{k+1}-a_{k}=c_{\Delta},

where

\displaystyle n_{\Delta}=\left\lfloor\frac{2\Lambda/N}{\Delta}\right\rfloor,\,c_{\Delta}=\frac{2\Lambda/N}{n_{\Delta}}\geq\Delta,

requiring
2\Lambda/N\geq\Delta
so
2\Lambda\geq N\Delta
.
So, the intervals
I_{k}
have the same length, which is at least
\Delta
and is asymptotically equal to
\Delta
as
N\Delta/\Lambda\to 0
.
Note that

\displaystyle N\Delta-1\leq\#\sigma(A_{r})\cap[a_{k},a_{k+1})

(9.7)

and we require that
N\Delta-1\geq 3
so
N\Delta\geq 4
.

We now move to calculating the various estimates in Lemma
8.3.4
.

Estimating
D_{I_{k}}
: There are two types of intervals
I=I_{k}
. If
n_{\Delta}
is odd, then
I_{(n_{\Delta}+1)/2}=[-c_{\Delta}/2,c_{\Delta}/2]
. All other intervals are of the form
[-b,-b+c_{\Delta}]
or
[b-c_{\Delta},b]
for
b\geq c_{\Delta}
.

We first deal with the exceptional case. Recall that
\sigma(A_{r})
consists of
-\lambda_{r}/N,\dots,\lambda_{r}/N
. So, the sets
\sigma(A_{r})
are nested consecutive and symmetric intervals in
\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}
.
Recall that
r_{0}=\min\mathscr{R}_{I}
is the smallest
r
so that
\sigma(A_{r})
contains
\sigma(A)\cap I
. We then bound

D_{I}\leq\max_{i}c_{i}^{r_{0}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{r_{0}}+1/2}{N}

by Lemma
5.1.1
(i)
.
If
r_{0}=1
, then we obtain

D_{I}\leq\frac{\lambda_{1}+1/2}{N}.

So, suppose that
r_{0}>1
. Because

\frac{\lambda_{r_{0}-1}}{N}<c_{\Delta}/2\leq\frac{\lambda_{r_{0}}}{N}

and
\lambda_{r_{0}}\leq\lambda_{r_{0}-1}+L
, we see that
\lambda_{r_{0}}\leq Nc_{\Delta}/2+L
. So,

D_{I}\leq\frac{Nc_{\Delta}/2+L+1/2}{N}=\frac{c_{\Delta}}{2}+\frac{L+1/2}{N}.

So, suppose that
I
is not the central interval of the previous case.
If
r_{0}=1
we apply the same bound as before. So, suppose that
r_{0}>1
.
If
I=[-b,-b+c_{\Delta}]
or
I=[b-c_{\Delta},b]
then

\frac{\lambda_{r_{0}-1}}{N}<b\leq\frac{\lambda_{r_{0}}}{N}.

Because
\lambda_{r_{0}}\leq\lambda_{r_{0}-1}+L
, we obtain

\lambda_{r_{0}}-N|x|\leq L+Nc_{\Delta},\;x\in I.

So, suppose
x=|i|/N\in I
so that
i\in[-\lambda_{r_{0}},\lambda_{r_{0}}]
.
Using
M=L+Nc_{\Delta}
in Lemma
5.1.1
(ii)
, we have

d_{\lambda_{r_{0}},i}\leq\sqrt{2\lambda_{r_{0}}(M+1)}\leq\sqrt{2\Lambda(L+Nc_{\Delta}+1)}

and hence

c_{i}^{r_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{N}\sqrt{2\Lambda(L+Nc_{\Delta}+1)}.

Therefore, we obtain the bound from the statement of the lemma:
D_{I}\leq D
.

Estimating
G_{I}
:
Note that in order to apply Lemma
8.3.4
, we need
(2m-3)(N_{0}+1)+4\leq\#\sigma(A)\cap I
, where we choose
N_{k}=N_{0}
for all
k
. The definition of

N_{0}
in the statement of the lemma was made to satisfy this inequality through Equation (
9.7
).

Estimating
G_{I}
involves estimating the sum of
c^{r+1}_{i}-c^{r}_{i}
and
\frac{\pi}{2N_{0}}\max(c^{r+1}_{i},c^{r}_{i})
. Using Lemma
5.1.1
(iv)
and
\lambda_{r+1}\leq\Lambda
, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle|c^{r+1}_{i}-c^{r}_{i}|

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi}{2N_{0}}\max(c^{r+1}_{i},c^{r}_{i})\leq G.

Estimating Equation (
8.15
)
:
By Lemma
5.1.1
(vi)
,

\|[S^{\ast},S]\|\leq\frac{2\Lambda}{N^{2}}.

By Lemma
5.1.1
(v)
, for all the weights

|(c^{r+1}_{i})^{2}-(c^{r}_{i})^{2}|\leq\frac{2\Lambda L}{N^{2}}.

By Lemma
5.1.1
(i)
,
\|S\|\leq(\Lambda+1/2)/N
. Note that we require
\alpha\leq 1/2
so that
1-2\alpha\geq 0
.
The desired estimate then follows from the estimates of
\|S^{\prime}-S\|
and
\|S^{\prime\prime}-S^{\prime}\|
from Lemma
8.3.4
.

When using
\alpha=1/3,C_{1/3}=5.3308
, we have
A^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
real so
\operatorname{Re}(S^{\prime\prime})
and
i\operatorname{Im}(S^{\prime\prime})
are as well. We now collect what we showed into the statement of the lemma.
∎

Example 9.1.2
.

We assume that the constants in the statement of Lemma
9.1.1
satisfy the asymptotic estimates

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\leq c_{0}N^{\gamma_{0}},m-1\leq c_{1}N^{\gamma_{1}},\underline{c_{2}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}}\leq\lambda_{m}\leq c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}},

\displaystyle L\leq c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}},l=c_{4}N^{\gamma_{4}},\Delta=c_{5}N^{-\gamma_{5}}.

(9.8)

We assume
N\geq N_{\ast}\geq 1
. Note that
N
will be an integer, though
N_{\ast}
is not assumed to be. Although we will prove more in this discussion, what we will use from it for Ogata’s theorem is expressed in Lemma
9.1.3
.

We now explore some mild assumptions on the exponents to obtain nearby commuting matrices using Lemma
9.1.1
.
First,
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3},\gamma_{5}>0
.
Because
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{m}
, we expect
\gamma_{0}\leq\gamma_{2}
.
Because
\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1}\leq(m-1)L
and often
\lambda_{1}=o(\lambda_{m})
, we will often have
\gamma_{2}\leq\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}
. For reasons explained below, we expect
\gamma_{1}\leq\gamma_{2}
as well. We will assume that
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
so that
N_{0}
can be large. To make the term coming from
\|S\|
bounded by a constant, we will assume that
\gamma_{2}\leq 1
.

The constants
l
and
\Delta
are chosen, while the others are given.
In particular,
l
will be chosen so that the first and fourth term in the estimate of
G
are equalized and negligible.
Because the optimal value of
l
is not a simple expression, we elect to choose
l
after the estimate for
G
is expressed in terms of the
c_{i}
,
\gamma_{i}
, and
N_{\ast}
.

Choosing the optimal constant and exponent for
\Delta
in this generality requires knowing more information about the relative sizes of the exponents in the definitions of
G
,
D
and
T
.
We make further assumptions about the exponents after having done as much simplification as possible.
The necessary condition
4\leq N\Delta\leq 2\Lambda
becomes

4\leq c_{5}N^{1-\gamma_{5}}\leq 2\underline{c_{2}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}}

4\leq c_{5}N^{1-\gamma_{5}},\;\;c_{5}\leq 2\underline{c_{2}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}-1}.

So, we further assume that
\gamma_{5}\leq 1
and
\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}\geq 1
.

We first find the optimal exponent for
\max(G,D)+C_{\alpha,\Lambda,N}\max(T^{\alpha},D^{2\alpha})
. Note with
\alpha\leq 1/2
, we will use
\Lambda\leq Const.N
so that
C_{\alpha,\Lambda,N}
is bounded by a constant.
It should be noted that we will not consider the asymptotics of
c_{\Delta}
for the matrix
A_{3}^{\prime}
during the optimization of the exponent because
D>c_{\Delta}/2
.

After finding the optimal exponent, we then bound all the terms by a constant factor multiplied by a single power of
N
. In particular, for
N\geq N_{\ast}
, all terms that are negligible will contribute to the constant factor in a way that depends on
N_{\ast}
as follows.
The primary inequality that will be used to choose optimal constant factors will be repeated applications of the following simple observation that if
a\geq b,N\geq N_{\ast}
then

N^{b}=N^{b-a}N^{a}\leq N_{\ast}^{b-a}N^{a}

In particular, if
a\geq 0
then

1\leq N_{\ast}^{-a}N^{a}.

We now proceed to the calculations.

c_{\Delta}
:
Because
x\mapsto x/(ax-b)
is decreasing as a function of
x>b/a
, we have

\displaystyle c_{\Delta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\lambda_{m}}{2\lambda_{m}\Delta^{-1}-N}\leq\frac{2\underline{c_{2}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}}}{\frac{2\underline{c_{2}}}{c_{5}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}}-N}\leq\frac{2\underline{c_{2}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}}}{\frac{2\underline{c_{2}}}{c_{5}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}}-N_{\ast}^{1-\underline{\gamma_{2}}-\gamma_{5}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}}}

\displaystyle=c_{5}\left(\frac{2\underline{c_{2}}}{2\underline{c_{2}}-c_{5}N_{\ast}^{1-\underline{\gamma_{2}}-\gamma_{5}}}\right)N^{-\gamma_{5}}=d_{\Delta}N^{-\gamma_{5}},

(9.9)

where we assume that
d_{\Delta}>0
(or equivalently
c_{5}<2\underline{c_{2}}N_{\ast}^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}-1}
).
Note that the upper bound for
c_{\Delta}
through that of
d_{\Delta}
is the only place in our calculations where we use the lower bound for
\lambda_{m}
. This guarantees that
\lambda_{m}
is much larger than
N\Delta
so that
c_{\Delta}
is approximately equal to
\Delta=c_{5}N^{-\gamma_{5}}
.

N_{0}
:

\displaystyle N_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c_{5}N^{-\gamma_{5}+1}-5}{2c_{1}N^{\gamma_{1}}-1}-2\geq\frac{c_{5}N^{-\gamma_{5}+1}-5N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{5}-1}N^{-\gamma_{5}+1}}{2c_{1}N^{\gamma_{1}}}-2N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}-1}N^{-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{5}+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{c_{5}-5N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{5}-1}}{2c_{1}}-2N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}-1}\right)N^{-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{5}+1}=d_{0}N^{-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{5}+1},

(9.10)

where we used the assumption that
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
. We further assume that
d_{0}>0
and
2c_{1}N^{\gamma_{1}}>1
.

T
:

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+\frac{2c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}}}{d_{0}N^{-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{5}+1}}\right)\frac{c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}}{N^{2}}=2c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}-2}+\frac{2c_{2}c_{3}}{d_{0}}N^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-3}

G
:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\max\left(\sqrt{c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}}\frac{2c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}}}{\sqrt{c_{4}}}N^{-\gamma_{4}/2}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}N^{-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{5}+1}}\left(c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\right),\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\sqrt{2c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}(c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}})}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}N^{-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{5}+1}}\sqrt{2c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}(c_{4}N^{\gamma_{4}}+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\max\left(2c_{3}\sqrt{\frac{c_{2}}{c_{4}}}N^{\gamma_{3}+(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{4})/2}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}N^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}-1}\left(c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{2}}N^{\gamma_{2}}\right),\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}}N^{(\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3})/2}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}N^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}-1}\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{4}N^{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{4}}+2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{4}}N^{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{4}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\max\left(2c_{3}\sqrt{\frac{c_{2}}{c_{4}}}N^{\gamma_{3}+(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{4})/2-1}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}\left(c_{2}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{2}}\right)N^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-2},\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}}N^{(\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3})/2-1}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{4}+2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{4}}}N^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}+(\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{4})/2-2}\right)

With the choice of
\gamma_{4}=-\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}-\gamma_{5}+1
, we equalize the exponents in the first and fourth terms, obtaining

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\leq\max\left(2c_{3}\sqrt{\frac{c_{2}}{c_{4}}}N^{(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-3)/2}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}\left(c_{2}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{2}}\right)N^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-2},\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}}N^{(\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3})/2-1}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{4}+2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{4}}}N^{(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-3)/2}\right).

Note that the first and fourth terms are not asymptotically larger than the third term because
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
. Later we will have a strict inequality so that these two terms become negligible as
N\to\infty
.

D
:

\displaystyle D\leq\max

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{\frac{2c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}}}{N}\left(\frac{c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}}+1}{N}+d_{\Delta}N^{-\gamma_{5}}\right)},\frac{1}{N}\left(c_{0}N^{\gamma_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\right),\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\frac{d_{\Delta}}{2}N^{-\gamma_{5}}+\frac{c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}}+1/2}{N}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}N^{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}-2}+2c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}-2}+2c_{2}d_{\Delta}N^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{5}-1}},c_{0}N^{\gamma_{0}-1}+\frac{1}{2}N^{-1},\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\frac{d_{\Delta}}{2}N^{-\gamma_{5}}+c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}-1}+\frac{1}{2}N^{-1}\right).

Note that the first term in the bound for
D
has three components, the first of which is asymptotically equal to the third term of
G
, considering the square root.

Optimal Asymptotics
:

Recall that
\alpha\leq 2\alpha\leq 1
. So, the slowest decaying term of
\max(G,D)+C_{\alpha,\Lambda,N}\max(T^{\alpha},D^{2\alpha})
has exponent

\displaystyle-\gamma

\displaystyle=\max\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-2,2\alpha\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}}{2}-1\right),2\alpha\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{5}-1}{2}\right),2\alpha(\gamma_{0}-1),\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.-2\alpha\gamma_{5},2\alpha(\gamma_{3}-1),\alpha(\gamma_{2}-2),\alpha(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-3)^{{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}|}}\right).

So,
-\gamma
is the largest of several exponents that, minimally, we wish to choose to be negative. We will then minimize
-\gamma
. Note that its optimal value will depend on
\alpha
as well as the appropriate choice of the
\gamma_{i}
.

We now impose additional assumptions on the exponents
\gamma_{i}
.
We further assume that we have
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{3}
. So, we assume that
\gamma_{3}\leq\gamma_{2}
. This corresponds to having a bound for the spacing
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}
that is asymptotically equal to the bound of the average spacing
(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1})/(m-1)
if additionally
m-1\geq Const.N^{\gamma_{1}}
.

Substituting
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle-\gamma

\displaystyle=\max\left(2\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-2,\alpha\left(\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}-2\right),\alpha\left(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{5}-1\right),2\alpha(\gamma_{0}-1),-2\alpha\gamma_{5},\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.2\alpha(\gamma_{3}-1),\alpha(\gamma_{2}-2),\alpha(2\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-3)\right).

(9.11)

Note that the requirement
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
becomes
\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
.

We now bound our estimates for
G,D,D^{2\alpha},T^{\alpha}
by a constant multiple of
N^{-\gamma}
. Note that by definition, if
a
is an exponent such that
a\leq-\gamma
then

N^{a}=N^{a+\gamma}N^{-\gamma}\leq N_{0}^{a+\gamma}N^{-\gamma}

since
a+\gamma\leq 0
.

So,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\leq\max\left(2c_{3}\sqrt{\frac{c_{2}}{c_{4}}}N_{\ast}^{\frac{2\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-3}{2}+\gamma}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}\left(c_{2}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{2}}\right)N_{\ast}^{2\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-2+\gamma},\right.

(9.12)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}}N_{\ast}^{\frac{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}}{2}-1+\gamma}+\frac{\pi}{2d_{0}}\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{4}+2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-2\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-1}}N_{\ast}^{\frac{2\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-3}{2}+\gamma}\right)N^{-\gamma},

\displaystyle D\leq\max

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}-2+2\gamma}+2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-2+2\gamma}+2c_{2}d_{\Delta}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{5}-1+2\gamma}}\right.,

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;c_{0}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{0}-1+\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-1+\gamma},

(9.13)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\frac{d_{\Delta}}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{5}+\gamma}+c_{3}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{3}-1+\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-1+\gamma}\right)N^{-\gamma},

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-2+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}+\frac{2c_{2}c_{3}}{d_{0}}N_{\ast}^{2\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{5}-3+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right)^{\alpha}N^{-\gamma},

(9.14)

\displaystyle D^{2\alpha}\leq\max

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{2c_{2}c_{3}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}-2+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}+2c_{2}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-2+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}+2c_{2}d_{\Delta}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{5}-1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}}\right.,

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;c_{0}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{0}-1+\frac{\gamma}{2\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-1+\frac{\gamma}{2\alpha}},

(9.15)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left.\frac{d_{\Delta}}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\gamma_{5}+\frac{\gamma}{2\alpha}}+c_{3}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{3}-1+\frac{\gamma}{2\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-1+\frac{\gamma}{2\alpha}}\right)^{2\alpha}N^{-\gamma}.

We write the result of the previous example as a lemma.

Lemma 9.1.3
.

Let
S=\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\lambda_{m}}
where
S^{\lambda}
is the irreducible
(2\lambda+1)
-dimensional spin representation of
su(2)
with
0\leq\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}\leq L
and the
2\lambda_{r}
are all even or all odd.

Suppose further that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\leq c_{0}N^{\gamma_{0}},m-1\leq c_{1}N^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}},\underline{c_{2}}N^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}}\leq\lambda_{m}\leq c_{2}N^{\gamma_{2}},

\displaystyle L\leq c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}},l=c_{4}N^{-\gamma_{2}+2\gamma_{3}-\gamma_{5}+1},\Delta=c_{5}N^{-\gamma_{5}},

where
\gamma_{i},c_{i},\underline{c_{2}}>0,\gamma_{0}<1,\gamma_{i},\underline{\gamma_{2}}\leq 1,\gamma_{3}\leq\gamma_{2}
,
\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}\geq 1
, and
\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
.
Suppose that the
c_{i}
and
N_{\ast}
satisfy the inequalities

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle<2c_{1}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}},\;\;4c_{1}N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-1}+5N_{\ast}^{\gamma_{5}-1}<c_{5},

\displaystyle 4

\displaystyle\leq c_{5}N_{\ast}^{1-\gamma_{5}},\;\;c_{5}<2\underline{c_{2}}N_{\ast}^{\underline{\gamma_{2}}+\gamma_{5}-1}

Let

C_{\alpha,\Lambda,N}=C_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\lambda_{m}+1/2}{N}\right)^{1-2\alpha}\leq Const.
, where
\alpha,C_{\alpha}
are as in Theorem
7.3.1
with additionally
\alpha\leq 1/2
. Let
d_{\Delta}
and
d_{0}
be defined by Equations (
9.9
) and (
9.10
) and let
\gamma=\gamma(\alpha,\gamma_{i})
be defined by Equation (
9.11
).

Then we have the bounds for
G,D,T^{\alpha},D^{2\alpha}
from Lemma
9.1.1
of the form

C(\alpha,c_{i},\gamma_{i},\underline{c_{2}},\underline{\gamma_{2}},N_{\ast})N^{-\gamma}
in Equations (
9.12
), (
9.1.2
), (
9.14
), and (
9.1.2
) so that there are commuting self-adjoint matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\|A_{1}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{1})\|,\|A_{2}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\max(G,D)+C_{\alpha,\Lambda,N}\max(T^{\alpha},D^{2\alpha})\leq Const.N^{-\gamma},

\displaystyle\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|

\displaystyle\leq d_{\Delta}N^{-\gamma_{5}}.

Moreover, when using
\alpha=1/3,C_{1/3}=5.3308
, we have that
A_{1}^{\prime},iA_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}
are real.

Example 9.1.4
.

With the set-up of the previous example, suppose that we are interested in the optimal exponent and the constant obtained as
N_{\ast}\to\infty
when
\alpha=1/3
.

For this example, we will assume that
c_{1}c_{3}\geq c_{2}
. In the next lemma below, we treat the details of this constraint which approximately holds when
N_{\ast}
is large,
\lambda_{1}=o(\lambda_{m})
, and
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}
is constant in
r
. Due to this assumption, we can easily remove the dependence of
c_{1}
as follows:
The only
occurrence of
c_{1}
in our inequalities is in
G
and
T
through
d_{0}^{-1}
.
We see that both
G
and
T
are decreased when
c_{1}
is decreased, so we choose
c_{1}=c_{2}/c_{3}
.

For this calculation, we assume that
\lambda_{m}=N/2
so that
\underline{c_{2}}=c_{2}=1/2,\underline{\gamma_{2}}=\gamma_{2}=1
. The condition
\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}\leq 1
then becomes
\gamma_{5}\leq\gamma_{3}
. We choose
\lambda_{1}=O(N^{1/2})
by taking
\gamma_{0}=1/2
.

For
\alpha=1/3
, the optimal choices of
\gamma_{3}=4/7,\gamma_{5}=3/7
give
\gamma=1/7
. Then the exponents in Equation (
9.11
) are

-\frac{1}{7},-\frac{1}{7},-\frac{1}{7},-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{2}{7},-\frac{2}{7},-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{4}{21}.

So, the slowest decaying terms have exponents
2\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{5}-2,\alpha(\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}-2),\alpha(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{5}-1)
which equal
-1/7
.
We note that as
N_{\ast}\to\infty
, we obtain that
d_{0}\sim c_{5}/(2c_{1})=c_{3}c_{5}/2c_{2},d_{\Delta}\sim c_{5}
.

So asymptotically,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi c_{2}}{2d_{0}}N^{-1/7}+o(N^{-1/7})=\frac{\pi}{4c_{3}c_{5}}N^{-1/7}+o(N^{-1/7}),

\displaystyle T^{1/3}

\displaystyle=o(N^{-1/7}),

\displaystyle D\ll D^{2/3}

\displaystyle\leq(2c_{2}c_{3}+2c_{2}d_{\Delta})^{1/3}N^{-1/7}+o(N^{-1/7})=(c_{3}+c_{5})^{1/3}N^{-1/7}+o(N^{-1/7}).

To approximately optimize our estimate of
\left(\frac{\pi}{4c_{3}c_{5}}+5.3308\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{1/3}(c_{3}+c_{5})^{1/3}\right)N^{-1/7}
, we choose
c_{3},c_{5}=0.95
. So, for
N
large, there are nearby commuting matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
satisfying the following inequalities

\displaystyle\|A_{1}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{1})\|,\|A_{2}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq 6.111\,N^{-\frac{1}{7}}

\displaystyle\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|

\displaystyle\leq 0.951\,N^{-\frac{3}{7}}

This estimate shows that we might as well assume that
N_{\ast}
is at least
(2\cdot 6.111)^{7}>4.07\times 10^{7}
. This is because
\|S(\sigma_{i})\|=\frac{1}{2}

so it is only when
N\geq(2\cdot 6.111)^{7}
that the obtained estimate is better than trivially choosing
A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{2}^{\prime}=0,A_{3}^{\prime}=A_{3}
.

We now prove the following lemma that is closer to what will be used for Ogata’s theorem. This result is a modification of the previous example that holds for all
N
.

Lemma 9.1.5
.

Let
N\geq 1
,
\Lambda_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{1/2}+\frac{3}{2}
, and
L=\lfloor 1.045\,N^{4/7}\rfloor
.
Let
S=\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\lambda_{m}}
with
\lambda_{1}\leq\Lambda_{0}+2L
,
\lambda_{m}\leq N/2
, and
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}=L
.

Then there are commuting self-adjoint matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\|A_{1}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{1})\|,\|A_{2}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq 6.286\,N^{-\frac{1}{7}},

\displaystyle\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|

\displaystyle\leq 1.083\,N^{-\frac{3}{7}}

and
A_{1}^{\prime},iA_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}
are real.

Proof.

Note that the variables
N_{\ast},c_{3},
and
\underline{c_{2}}
will be left undetermined until the end of the proof. We also at this point define
L=\lfloor c_{3}N^{4/7}\rfloor\leq c_{3}N^{\gamma_{3}}
with
\gamma_{3}=\frac{4}{7}
. We will obtain estimates for three cases then choose the optimal values for these constants to obtain the result of the lemma.

\lambda_{m}<\underline{c_{2}}N^{6/7}
:

This case only relies the value of the variable
\underline{c_{2}}
. By Equation (
5.2
) we have

\|S(\sigma_{i})\|<\frac{\underline{c_{2}}N^{6/7}}{N}=\underline{c_{2}}N^{-1/7}.

So, we may safely choose
A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{2}^{\prime}=0
and
A_{3}^{\prime}=S(\sigma_{3})
.
The estimates in the statement of the lemma that we obtain are
\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|=0
and for
i=1,2
,

\|A_{i}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{i})\|=\|S(\sigma_{i})\|<\underline{c_{2}}N^{-1/7}.

N<N_{\ast}
:

This case only relies the value of the variable
N_{\ast}
.

As in the previous case, we choose
A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{2}^{\prime}=0
and
A_{3}^{\prime}=S(\sigma_{3})
. Because
N^{1/7}<N_{\ast}^{1/7}
, we have

\|S(\sigma_{i})\|\leq\frac{N/2}{N}<\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{1/7}N^{-1/7}.

N\geq N_{\ast}
,
\underline{c_{2}}N^{6/7}\leq\lambda_{m}
:
This is the only non-trivial case and it relies on the values of
N_{\ast},c_{3},
and
\underline{c_{2}}
. Due to our use of Lemma
9.1.3
, we will also have other constants

We will apply Lemma
9.1.3
with exponents
\gamma_{0}=\frac{4}{7},\underline{\gamma_{2}}=\frac{6}{7},\gamma_{2}=1,\gamma_{3}=\frac{4}{7},\gamma_{5}=\frac{3}{7},\gamma=\frac{1}{7}
and with
c_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
.

First note that

\lambda_{0}\leq\Lambda_{0}+2L\leq 2c_{3}N^{\frac{4}{7}}+\frac{1}{2}N^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{3}{2}\leq\left(2c_{3}+\frac{1}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\frac{1}{14}}+\frac{3}{2}N_{\ast}^{-\frac{4}{7}}\right)N^{\frac{4}{7}}=c_{0}N^{\gamma_{0}}.

Also, because
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}
is constant, we see that

m-1=\frac{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{1}}{L}\leq\frac{\lambda_{m}}{c_{3}N^{\frac{4}{7}}-1}\leq\frac{N}{2c_{3}N^{\frac{4}{7}}-2N_{\ast}^{-\frac{4}{7}}N^{\frac{4}{7}}}=\frac{1}{2c_{3}-2N_{\ast}^{-\frac{4}{7}}}N^{\frac{3}{7}}=c_{1}N^{\gamma_{1}}.

Observe that the exponent provided here is
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}=1-\frac{4}{7}=\frac{3}{7}.

Choice of constants
: So, at this point we only need to choose the values for
\underline{c_{2}},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5},
and
N_{\ast}
for the estimate. We choose the approximately optimal
c_{3}=1.045,c_{4}=18.65,c_{5}=1.082,\underline{c_{2}}=6.285,
and
N_{\ast}=4.962\times 10^{7}
.
We then obtain the results of the lemma from all these cases, noting that the required conditions on the constants hold.
∎

9.2
Proof of Main Results

Example 9.2.1
.

Using the following example, we will illustrate how we prove our extension of
Ogata’s theorem (Theorem
1.3.1
) over the next two theorems. Consider the
scaled representation

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{28}\left(2S^{1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\oplus 4S^{2}\oplus 7S^{3}\oplus 8S^{4}\oplus 7S^{5}\oplus 6S^{6}\oplus 4S^{7}\oplus 4S^{8}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\oplus 4S^{9}\oplus 3S^{10}\oplus 3S^{11}\oplus 2S^{12}\oplus S^{13}\oplus S^{14}\right)

with multiplicities illustrated in Illustration
9.4
(a).
Recall that, just as in the next two results, the
1/28
is a multiplicative factor while the constant
n_{i}
of
n_{i}S^{\lambda_{i}}
indicates the multiplicity of
S^{\lambda_{i}}
in the (unscaled) representation
28S
.

Illustration 9.4:
Illustration of irreducible representations for Example
9.2.1
.

Illustration
9.4
(a) is a graph of the multiplicities of the irreducible representations in
S
. We construct the almost commuting matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
nearby the
S(\sigma_{i})
as follows.
We first partition the direct sum appropriately, which gives us subrepresentations acting on orthogonal invariant subspaces. For each of these subrepresentations we construct nearby commuting matrices.
Then the nearby commuting matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
are formed by taking the direct sum of the commuting matrices formed in all the invariant subspaces. The distance
\|A_{i}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{i})\|
will be the maximal distance in each of the invariant subspaces corresponding to the partition.

We now discuss the partitions and how we construct their nearby commuting matrices. First, refine the representations illustrated in (b) into two subsets illustrated with
\times
’s and
\ast
’s.
One such partition will correspond to the
\times
irreducible representations.
Because the spins of the
\times
representations are at most
3
, we will “discard” all of these by choosing trivial nearby commuting matrices as in the previous lemma. This provides an error of
\frac{3}{28}
.

We chose which representations were
\times
’s and
\ast
’s in such a way that the multiplicities of the
\ast
irreducible representations were monotonically decreasing. We then can form a “level set” decomposition illustrated by some long and some short horizontal boxes that group the
\ast
representations as in (b).

A sample horizontal grouping of representations is given in (c).
Each such horizontal grouping of representations will be itself partitioned as follows. We choose a value of
L
, which is
3
in this example. We partition each horizontal grouping of
\ast
representations so that the spins in each partition increase by exactly
L
. These are illustrated in (d).

The way that this is described in the proof of the Theorem
9.2.2
is by choosing the arithmetic progression of spins
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{K}
where
\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}=L
and
\mu_{K}
is one of the last
L
spins to the far right of the grouping in (c).
These provide the partitions of the
\ast
representations for which we obtain nearby commuting matrices by Lemma
9.1.5
.

Note that, strictly speaking, in order to apply Theorem
9.2.2
, we do not need the representations to be monotonically increasing in the sense that
n_{i}\geq n_{i+1}
after some point. What is actually needed is that the multiplicities are monotonically decreasing with steps of size
L
:
n_{i}\geq n_{i+L}
.

Theorem 9.2.2
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}
be given with
\lambda_{i_{\ast}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}+1
,
\lambda_{m}\leq\frac{1}{2}N
, and
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}=1
. Define
L=\lfloor 1.045\,N^{4/7}\rfloor
.

Let
S=\frac{1}{N}\left(n_{1}S^{\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus n_{m}S^{\lambda_{m}}\right)
, where
n_{i}\geq n_{i+L}
for
i\geq i_{\ast}
.

Then there are commuting self-adjoint matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\|A_{1}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{1})\|,\|A_{2}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq 6.286\,N^{-\frac{1}{7}},

\displaystyle\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|

\displaystyle\leq 1.083\,N^{-\frac{3}{7}}

and
A_{1}^{\prime},iA_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}
are real.

The same result applies if instead
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}=1/2
.

Proof.

We first relabel the indices of the weights so that
i_{\ast}=1
and the weights are
\lambda_{i}
for
i_{0}\leq i\leq m
with
i_{0}\leq 1
being possibly negative. To avoid the trivial case, we can assume that
N\geq(2\cdot 6.2)^{7}\approx 4.5\times 10^{7}
.

Because the differences
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}
are an integer, all the
\lambda_{r}
are integers or half-integers.
If we had instead
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}=1/2
then we decompose
S
into a direct sum of the representations with
\lambda_{r}
integers and
\lambda_{r}^{\prime}
half-integers and apply the construction for each separately with
\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}+1,\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}+\frac{3}{2}
.
So, we assume that
\lambda_{r+1}-\lambda_{r}=1
and
\lambda_{1}\leq\Lambda_{0}
, where
\Lambda_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}+\frac{3}{2}
.

We now break the representation into subrepresentations as follows. If
\lambda_{m}
is an integer, let
Z
be the set of integers. If
\lambda_{m}
is a half-integer, let
Z
be the set of half-integers. Then the collection of all
\lambda_{r}
is equal to
[\lambda_{i_{0}},\lambda_{m}]\cap Z
. We first partition
[\lambda_{i_{0}},\lambda_{m}]\cap Z
into

[\lambda_{i_{0}},\Lambda_{0}+2L)\cap Z
and
[\Lambda_{0}+2L,\lambda_{m}]\cap Z
.

For each
\mu_{K}\in(\lambda_{m}-L,\lambda_{m}]\cap Z
, we form a disjoint (with indices relabeled) arithmetic progression
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{K}
, where
\Lambda_{0}+L<\mu_{1}\leq\Lambda_{0}+2L
and
\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}=L
. The set
[\Lambda_{0}+2L,\lambda_{m}]\cap Z
is thus contained in the union of these disjoint arithmetic progressions.

We now focus on forming nearby commuting self-adjoint matrices for subrepresentations of the representation
N\cdot S
corresponding to the arithmetic progressions and also to the representations not accounted for by one of the arithmetic progressions. Then the desired matrices
A_{i}^{\prime}
are formed from the appropriate direct sums.

Suppose
\lambda_{j}\in[\lambda_{i_{0}},\Lambda_{0}+2L)\cap Z
does not belong to one of the above constructed arithmetic progressions. Then

\lambda_{j}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}+\frac{3}{2}+2L\leq 5N^{\frac{4}{7}},

hence

\|S^{\lambda_{j}}(\sigma_{i})\|\leq\frac{5}{N}N^{\frac{4}{7}}=5\,N^{-\frac{3}{7}}.

So, on this summand we choose the component of
A_{1}^{\prime}
and of
A_{2}^{\prime}
to be zero and the component of
A_{3}^{\prime}
to be
\frac{1}{N}S^{\lambda_{j}}(\sigma_{3})
. This guarantees a contribution of at most
5N^{-3/7}
to
\|A_{1}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{1})\|
and
\|A_{2}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{2})\|
on this summand and no contribution to
\|A_{3}^{\prime}-S(\sigma_{3})\|
on this summand.

Now, consider one of the above constructed arithmetic progression
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{K}
. For simplicity of notation, let
n_{\mu}
be the multiplicity of the representation
S^{\mu}
in the representation
N\cdot S
. Then

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{K}n_{\mu_{i}}S^{\mu_{i}}=n_{\mu_{K}}\left(S^{\mu_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\mu_{K}}\right)\oplus\bigoplus_{r=2}^{K}(n_{\mu_{r-1}}-n_{\mu_{r}})(S^{\mu_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\mu_{r-1}}).

This is well-defined because
n_{i}-n_{i+L}\geq 0
so
n_{\mu_{r-1}}-n_{\mu_{r}}\geq 0
.

So, we focus on obtaining nearby commuting matrices for the representation of the form
S^{\mu_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus S^{\mu_{r}}
.
Nearby commuting matrices are obtained by applying Lemma
9.1.5
since
\mu_{1}\leq\Lambda_{0}+2L,\mu_{K}\leq\lambda_{m}\leq\frac{1}{2}N,\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}=L
.
So, we conclude the proof of the lemma by taking direct sums of the nearby commuting matrices obtained in each summand.
∎

We now prove Theorem
1.3.1
, giving a constructive proof of Ogata’s Theorem for
d=2
with an explicit estimate and additional structure.

Proof of Theorem
1.3.1
.

We begin with the first statement. Consider the representation
(S^{1/2})^{\otimes N}
decomposed as a direct sum of irreducible representations as discussed in Chapter
5
. We write

(S^{1/2})^{\otimes N}\cong n_{0}S^{0}\oplus n_{1/2}S^{1/2}\oplus\cdots\oplus n_{N/2}S^{N/2}.

As discussed in Chapter
5
, this decomposition as well as the unitary operator on
M_{2^{N}}(\mathbb{C})
that realizes this equivalence can be obtained constructively. Moreover, we choose the unitary to be real.

Depending on whether
N
is even or odd, the
n_{\lambda}
are only non-zero when the
\lambda
are all integers or are all half-integers, respectively. By Lemma
5.2.3
, we know that
n_{\lambda}\geq n_{\lambda+1}
for
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{N}
. So, we apply Theorem
9.2.2
with

T_{N}=\frac{1}{N}(S^{1/2})^{\otimes N}\cong\frac{1}{N}\left(n_{0}S^{0}\oplus n_{1/2}S^{1/2}\oplus\cdots\oplus n_{N/2}S^{N/2}\right)

to obtain commuting real self-adjoint matrices
Y_{1,N},iY_{2,N},Y_{3,N}
that satisfy

\|T_{N}(\sigma_{i})-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 6.286\,N^{-\frac{1}{7}}

for
i=1,2
and

\|T_{N}(\sigma_{3})-Y_{3,N}\|\leq 1.083\,N^{-\frac{3}{7}}.

To obtain the estimate for a more general operator, we proceed as discussed in Section
1.5
. If
A
is given by
c_{1}\sigma_{1}+c_{2}\sigma_{2}+c_{3}\sigma_{3}+c_{4}I_{2}
then define
Y_{N}(A)=c_{1}Y_{1,N}+c_{2}Y_{2,N}+c_{3}Y_{3,N}+c_{4}I_{2^{N}}
. Recall that
T_{N}(I_{2})=I_{2^{N}}
and by Equation (
1.3
),

\sqrt{|c_{1}|^{2}+|c_{2}|^{2}+|c_{3}|^{2}}\leq 2\|A\|.

So, by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality,

\displaystyle\|T_{N}(A)-Y_{N}(A)\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{3}|c_{i}|\|T_{N}(\sigma_{i})-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 6.286(|c_{1}|+|c_{2}|)N^{-\frac{1}{7}}+1.083|c_{3}|N^{-\frac{3}{7}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2(6.286^{2})+1.083^{2}N^{-4/7}}\|A\|N^{-\frac{1}{7}}\leq 17.92\|A\|N^{-1/7}.

Recall that by Equation (
5.1
),

\sigma_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\;\;\sigma_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
-i&0\end{pmatrix},\;\;\sigma_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

So,
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3},I_{2}
are symmetric self-adjoint
2\times 2
matrices and
\sigma_{2}
is an antisymmetric self-adjoint matrix. Because
Y_{1,N},Y_{3,N},Y_{N}(I_{2})
are real and self-adjoint, they are symmetric. Because
Y_{2,N}
is imaginary and self-adjoint, it is antisymmetric. Therefore,

Y_{N}(A^{\ast})=Y_{N}(\overline{c_{1}}\sigma_{1}+\overline{c_{2}}\sigma_{2}+\overline{c_{3}}\sigma_{3}+\overline{c_{4}}I_{2})=\overline{c_{1}}Y_{1,N}+\overline{c_{2}}Y_{2,N}+\overline{c_{3}}Y_{3,N}+\overline{c_{4}}Y_{I,N}=Y_{N}(A)^{\ast}

and

Y_{N}(A^{T})=Y_{N}(c_{1}\sigma_{1}-c_{2}\sigma_{2}+c_{3}\sigma_{3}+c_{4}I_{2})=c_{1}Y_{1,N}-c_{2}Y_{2,N}+c_{3}Y_{3,N}+c_{4}Y_{I,N}=Y_{N}(A)^{T}.

The theorem then follow from these observations.
∎

Remark 9.2.3
.

For a 3 dimensional grid of
10^{5}
particles along each axis, one sees that
N=10^{15}
is a reasonable value of
N
to apply our result to. We then would have the estimates

\|T_{N}(\sigma_{i})-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 0.046
and for more general operators
\|T_{N}(A)-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 0.13\|A\|
.

For
N=(10^{7})^{3}
,

\|T_{N}(\sigma_{i})-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 0.0063
and
\|T_{N}(A)-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 0.018\|A\|
.

For
N=(10^{10})^{3}
,

\|T_{N}(\sigma_{i})-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 0.00033
and
\|T_{N}(A)-Y_{i,N}\|\leq 0.00093\|A\|
.

Remark 9.2.4
.

Loring and Sørensen in
[
86
]
extend Lin’s theorem to respect real matrices. They show that two almost commuting real self-adjoint matrices are nearby commuting real self-adjoint matrices. We have shown that this result is true for Ogata’s theorem for
d=2
.
The result that we found of the additional structure for
Y_{i,N}
corresponds to what
[
96
]
calls Class D in 2D (Section 5.2), which is the case of two real self-adjoint matrices and one imaginary self-adjoint matrix that are almost commuting and for which we want to find nearby commuting approximants with the same structure.

It should be remarked that the suboptimal exponent
\alpha=1/3
was used because it provided the real structure of the
A_{i}^{\prime}
and a small explicit constant
C_{1/3}
. Using
\alpha=\frac{1}{2},\gamma_{3}=\frac{3}{5},\gamma_{5}=\frac{2}{5},\gamma=\frac{1}{5}
and similar arguments as above, one can obtain the following result. Because
C_{1/2}\leq C_{KS}
is undetermined we state this result with the best asymptotic decay that our method provides but without an explicit constant.

Theorem 9.2.5
.

There is a linear map
Y_{N}:M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to M_{2^{N}}(\mathbb{C})
such that the
Y_{N}(A)
commute for all
A\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,

Y_{N}(A^{\ast})=Y_{N}(A)^{\ast},

and

\|T_{N}(A)-Y_{N}(A)\|\leq Const.\|A\|\,N^{-1/5}.

Consequently,
Y_{N}
preserves the property of being self-adjoint or skew-adjoint.
